      <li class="git-code-line" id="L110" data-git-line="110">
        <button
          type="button"

          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 110"
          title="Copy permalink for line 110"
        >110</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h5&gt;3.1 The Practice of Cybersecurity&lt;/h5&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L111" data-git-line="111">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="111"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 111"
          title="Copy permalink for line 111"
        >111</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;An important aspect of security is that it usually involves reasoning under uncertainty. Although we have plenty of deductive skills, we are by no means mentally omniscient. We cannot determine everything that follows from a given truth, and we cannot know or remember an infinite number of facts.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L112" data-git-line="112">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="112"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 112"
          title="Copy permalink for line 112"
        >112</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;There are a few distinct characteristics of cybersecurity that distinguish it from other technical fields. First, security involves &lt;strong&gt;malicious and intelligent actors (i.e. opponents)&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L113" data-git-line="113">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="113"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 113"
          title="Copy permalink for line 113"
        >113</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The problem of the intelligent adversary and the problem of uncertainty both suggest that understanding cybersecurity necessitates learning more about how we think as human agents, and how to solve problems. This means we&amp;#39;ll need to adopt and nurture specific mindsets that will help us as we learn and apply our skills.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L114" data-git-line="114">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="114"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 114"
          title="Copy permalink for line 114"
        >114</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Another important aspect of security is that it usually involves &lt;strong&gt;reasoning under uncertainty&lt;/strong&gt;. Although we have plenty of deductive skills, we are by no means mentally omniscient. We cannot determine everything that follows from a given truth, and we cannot know or remember an infinite number of facts.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L115" data-git-line="115">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="115"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 115"
          title="Copy permalink for line 115"
        >115</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Another extremely valuable mindset is the aptly-coined &lt;strong&gt;security mindset. Proposed by security researcher Bruce Schneier&lt;/strong&gt;,2 this mindset encourages a constant questioning of how one can attack (or defend) a system. If we can begin to ask this question automatically when encountering a novel idea, machine, system, network, or object, we can start noticing a wide array of recurring patterns.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L116" data-git-line="116">

      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L117" data-git-line="117">

      </li>

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Risk - A simple way to define risk is to consider two axes: the probability that a negative event will occur, and the impact on something we value if such an event happens. This definition allows us to conceptualize risks via four quadrants:&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L137" data-git-line="137">
        <button

        <button

        >143</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&amp;quot;How likely is it that a particular attack might happen?&amp;quot; and &amp;quot;What would be the worst possible outcome if the attack occurs?&amp;quot;&lt;/p&gt;</span>

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L144" data-git-line="144">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="144"

          title="Copy permalink for line 144"
        >144</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;When we can attribute a specific risk to a particular cause, we&amp;#39;re describing a threat. In cybersecurity, a threat2 is something that poses risk to an asset we care about protecting. Not all threats are human; if our network depends on the local electricity grid, a severe lightning storm could be a threat to ongoing system operations.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L145" data-git-line="145">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="145"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 145"
          title="Copy permalink for line 145"
        >145</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;A person or group of people embodying a threat is known as a threat actor,3 a term signifying agency, motivation, and intelligence.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L146" data-git-line="146">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="146"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 146"
          title="Copy permalink for line 146"
        >146</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;For a threat to become an actual risk, the target being threatened must be vulnerable in some manner. A vulnerability4 is a flaw that allows a threat to cause harm. Not all flaws are vulnerabilities. To take a non-security example, let&amp;#39;s imagine a bridge. A bridge can have some aesthetic flaws; maybe some pavers are scratched or it isn&amp;#39;t perfectly straight. However, these flaws aren&amp;#39;t vulnerabilities because they don&amp;#39;t pose any risk of damage to the bridge. Alternatively, if the bridge does have structural flaws in its construction, it may be vulnerable to specific threats such as overloading or too much wind.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L147" data-git-line="147">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="147"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 147"
          title="Copy permalink for line 147"
        >147</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;In December 20215, a vulnerability was discovered in the Apache Log4J6 library, a popular Java-based logging library. This vulnerability could lead to arbitrary code execution by taking advantage of a JNDI Java toolkit feature which, by default, allowed for download requests to enrich logging. If a valid Java file was downloaded, this program would be executed by the server. This means that if user-supplied input (such as a username or HTTP header) was improperly sanitized before being logged, it was possible to make the server download a malicious Java file that would allow a remote, unauthorized user to execute commands on the server.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L148" data-git-line="148">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="148"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 148"
          title="Copy permalink for line 148"
        >148</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Due to the popularity of the Log4j library, this vulnerability was given the highest possible rating under the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS)7 used to score vulnerabilities: 10.0 Critical. This rating led to a frenzied aftermath including vendors, companies, and individuals scrambling to identify and patch vulnerable systems as well as search for indications of compromise. Additional Log4J vulnerabilities were discovered soon after, exacerbating matters.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L149" data-git-line="149">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="149"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 149"
          title="Copy permalink for line 149"
        >149</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;This vulnerability could have been prevented by ensuring that user-supplied data is properly sanitized.8 The issue could have been mitigated by ensuring that potentially dangerous features (such as allowing web-requests and code execution) were disabled by default.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L150" data-git-line="150">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="150"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 150"
          title="Copy permalink for line 150"
        >150</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;An attack surface10 describes all the points of contact on our system or network that could be vulnerable to exploitation. An attack vector11 is a specific vulnerability and exploitation combination that can further a threat actor&amp;#39;s objectives.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L151" data-git-line="151">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="151"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 151"
          title="Copy permalink for line 151"
        >151</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Threats&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L152" data-git-line="152">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="152"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 152"
          title="Copy permalink for line 152"
        >152</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Individual Malicious Actors:&lt;/strong&gt; On the most superficial level, anyone attempting to do something that they are not supposed to do fits into this category. In cybersecurity, malicious actors can explore digital tactics that are unintended by developers, such as authenticating to restricted services, stealing credentials, and defacing websites.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L153" data-git-line="153">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="153"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 153"
          title="Copy permalink for line 153"
        >153</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Malicious Groups:&lt;/strong&gt; When individuals band together to form groups, they often become stronger than their individual group members. This can be even more true online because the ability to communicate instantly and at vast distances enables people to achieve goals that would have been impossible without such powerful communication tools. For example, the ability to quickly coordinate on who-does-what over a instant messaging services is just as valuable to malicious cyber groups as it is to modern businesses. Malicious groups can have any number of goals, but are usually more purposeful, organized, and resourceful than individuals. Thus, they are often considered to be one of the more dangerous threat actors.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L154" data-git-line="154">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="154"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 154"
          title="Copy permalink for line 154"
        >154</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Insider Threats:&lt;/strong&gt; Perhaps one of the most dangerous types of threat actor, an insider threat is anyone who already has privileged access to a system and can abuse their privileges to attack it. Often, insider threats are individuals or groups of employees or ex-employees of an enterprise that become motivated to harm it in some capacity. Insider threats can be so treacherous because they are usually assumed to have a certain level of trust. That trust can be exploited to gain further access to resources, or these actors may simply have access to internal knowledge that isn&amp;#39;t meant to be public.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L155" data-git-line="155">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="155"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 155"
          title="Copy permalink for line 155"
        >155</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Nation States:&lt;/strong&gt; Although international cyber politics, cyber war, and digital intelligence are vast subjects and significantly beyond the scope of this Module, we should recognize that some of the most proficient, resourceful, and well-financed operators of cyber attacks exist at the nation-state level within many different countries across the globe.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L156" data-git-line="156">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="156"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 156"
          title="Copy permalink for line 156"
        >156</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Attacks&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L157" data-git-line="157">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="157"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 157"
          title="Copy permalink for line 157"
        >157</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Social Engineering:&lt;/strong&gt; Social Engineering represents a broad class of attacks where an attacker persuades or manipulates human victims to provide them with information or access that they shouldn&amp;#39;t have.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L158" data-git-line="158">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="158"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 158"
          title="Copy permalink for line 158"
        >158</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phishing:&lt;/strong&gt; Phishing is a more general class of attack relative to spearphishing. While spearphishing attacks are targeted to specific individuals, phishing is usually done in broad sweeps. Phishing strategy is usually to try to send a malicious communication to as many people as possible, inreasing the likelihood of a victim clicking a link or otherwise doing something that would compromise security.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L159" data-git-line="159">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="159"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 159"
          title="Copy permalink for line 159"
        >159</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The United States Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI)5 recommends these and other steps be taken to prevent BEC:&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L160" data-git-line="160">
        <button
          type="button"

          data-git-line-anchor="160"

        >160</button>

        >168</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ransomware:&lt;/strong&gt; Ransomware is a type of malware that infects computer systems and then locks a legitimate user from accessing it properly. Often, users are contacted by the attacker and asked for a ransom in order to unlock their machine or documents.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L169" data-git-line="169">

        >169</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Credential Abuse:&lt;/strong&gt; Credential Abuse can occur when an attacker acquires legitimate credentials, allowing them to log into machines or services that they otherwise would not be able to. Often, attackers are able to guess user passwords because they are predictable or weak.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L170" data-git-line="170">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Authentication Bypass:&lt;/strong&gt; While Credential Abuse allows attackers to log in to services by legitimate means, Authentication Bypasses can allow attackers to ignore or step-around intended authentication protocols.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L171" data-git-line="171">
        <button
          type="button"

        >171</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h5&gt;3.3 The CIA Triad&lt;/h5&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L172" data-git-line="172">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="172"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 172"
          title="Copy permalink for line 172"
        >172</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;One of the models often used to describe the relationship between security and its objects is known the CIA triad. CIA stands for Confidentiality, Integrity, and Availability. Each of these is a desirable property of the things we might want to secure, and each of these three properties can be attacked. Most (though not all) attacks against computer systems and networks will threaten one of these attributes. Let&amp;#39;s begin with a high level overview before we dive into each one:&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L173" data-git-line="173">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="173"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 173"
          title="Copy permalink for line 173"
        >173</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;ul&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L174" data-git-line="174">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="174"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 174"
          title="Copy permalink for line 174"
        >174</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Confidentiality: Can actors who should not have access to the system or information access the system or information?&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L175" data-git-line="175">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="175"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 175"
          title="Copy permalink for line 175"
        >175</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Integrity: Can the data or the system be modified in some way that is not intended?&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L176" data-git-line="176">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="176"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 176"
          title="Copy permalink for line 176"
        >176</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Availability: Are the data or the system accessible when and how they are intended to be?&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L177" data-git-line="177">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="177"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 177"
          title="Copy permalink for line 177"
        >177</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;/ul&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L178" data-git-line="178">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"

          title="Copy permalink for line 178"
        >178</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Confidentiality&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L179" data-git-line="179">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="179"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 179"
          title="Copy permalink for line 179"
        >179</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;A system is Confidential if the only people that can access it are the people explicitly permitted to do so. A person&amp;#39;s social media account credentials are considered confidential as long as the user&amp;#39;s password is known only to the owner. If a hacker steals or guesses the password and they can access the account, this would constitute an attack against confidentiality. Common attacks against confidentiality include network eavesdropping1 and credential stuffing.2&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L180" data-git-line="180">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="180"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 180"
          title="Copy permalink for line 180"
        >180</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_eavesdropping&quot;&gt;Network Eavesdropping&lt;/a&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L181" data-git-line="181">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="181"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 181"
          title="Copy permalink for line 181"
        >181</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credential_stuffing&quot;&gt;Credential Stuffing&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L182" data-git-line="182">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="182"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 182"
          title="Copy permalink for line 182"
        >182</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Integrity&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L183" data-git-line="183">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="183"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 183"
          title="Copy permalink for line 183"
        >183</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;A system has Integrity if the information and functionality it stores is only that which the owner intends to be stored. Integrity is concerned with maintaining the accuracy and reliability of data and services. Merely logging on to a user&amp;#39;s social media account by guessing their password is not an attack against integrity. However, if the attacker starts to post messages or delete information, this would become an integrity attack as well. A common attack against integrity is arbitrary code execution.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L184" data-git-line="184">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="184"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 184"
          title="Copy permalink for line 184"
        >184</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbitrary_code_execution&quot;&gt;Arbitrary Code Execution&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L185" data-git-line="185">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="185"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 185"
          title="Copy permalink for line 185"
        >185</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Availability&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L186" data-git-line="186">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="186"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 186"
          title="Copy permalink for line 186"
        >186</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;A system is considered Available if the people who are supposed to access it can do so. Imagine an attacker has gained access to a social media account and also posted some content of their choosing. So far, this would constitute an attack against confidentiality and integrity. If the attacker changes the user&amp;#39;s password and prevents them from logging on, this would also become an attack against availability. A common attack against availability is denial of service.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L187" data-git-line="187">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"

      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L188" data-git-line="188">

        >195</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h5&gt;3.4 Security Principles, Controls, and Strategies&lt;/h5&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L196" data-git-line="196">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="196"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 196"
          title="Copy permalink for line 196"
        >196</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;ul&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L197" data-git-line="197">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="197"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 197"
          title="Copy permalink for line 197"
        >197</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Understand the importance of multiple layers of defense in a security strategy.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L198" data-git-line="198">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="198"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 198"
          title="Copy permalink for line 198"
        >198</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Describe threat intelligence and its applications in an organization.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L199" data-git-line="199">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="199"

        >199</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Learn why access and user privileges should be restricted as much as possible.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L200" data-git-line="200">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Understand why security should not depend on secrecy.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L201" data-git-line="201">
        <button

        >201</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Identify policies that can mitigate threats to an organization.&lt;/li&gt;</span>

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L204" data-git-line="204">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="204"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 204"
          title="Copy permalink for line 204"
        >204</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Security Principles&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L205" data-git-line="205">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="205"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 205"
          title="Copy permalink for line 205"
        >205</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principle_of_least_privilege&quot;&gt;Principle of Least Privilege&lt;/a&gt; expresses the idea that each part within a system should only be granted the lowest possible privileges needed to achieve its task. Whether referring to users on a machine or lines of code in a program, correctly adhering to this discipline can greatly narrow the attack surface.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L206" data-git-line="206">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="206"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 206"
          title="Copy permalink for line 206"
        >206</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero_trust_security_model&quot;&gt;Zero Trust&lt;/a&gt; security model takes the Principle of Least Privilege and carries it to its ultimate conclusion. This model advocates for removing all implicit trust of networks and has a goal of protecting access to resources, often with granular authorization processes for every resource request.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L207" data-git-line="207">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="207"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 207"
          title="Copy permalink for line 207"
        >207</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_security&quot;&gt;Open Security&lt;/a&gt;, a somewhat counter-intuitive principle, states that the security of a system should not depend on its secrecy. In other words, even if an attacker knows exactly how the system&amp;#39;s security is implemented, the attacker should still be thwarted. This isn&amp;#39;t to say that nothing should be secret. Credentials are a clear case where the security of a password depends on its secrecy. However, we&amp;#39;d want our system to be secure even if the attacker knows there is a password, and even if they know the cryptographic algorithm behind it.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L208" data-git-line="208">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="208"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 208"
          title="Copy permalink for line 208"
        >208</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_in_depth_%28computing%29&quot; title=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_in_depth_(computing)&quot;&gt;Defense in Depth&lt;/a&gt; advocates for adding defenses to as many layers of a system as possible, so that if one is bypassed, another may still prevent full infiltration. An example of defense in depth outside the context of cybersecurity would be a garage that requires entering an electronic code, using a key on a bolted door lock, then finally disabling a voice-activated internal alarm system to open the garage.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>

        >222</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Shift-Left Security&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L223" data-git-line="223">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="223"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 223"
          title="Copy permalink for line 223"
        >223</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;One of the best ways to avoid extra costs and impacts to availability is to design an entire system so that security is built into the service architecture, rather than requiring many additional software layers. In order to design systems with built-in security, the idea of &lt;a href=&quot;https://devopedia.org/shift-left&quot;&gt;shift-left security&lt;/a&gt; can improve efficiency. The idea of shift-left security is to consider security engineering from the outset when designing a product or system, rather than attempt to bake it in after the product has been built.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L224" data-git-line="224">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="224"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 224"
          title="Copy permalink for line 224"
        >224</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Administrative Segmentation&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L225" data-git-line="225">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="225"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 225"
          title="Copy permalink for line 225"
        >225</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;It may seem okay to have an administrator bypass security controls based on their role and functional needs. Shouldn&amp;#39;t we trust our administrators? However, when a threat is internal or otherwise able to obtain valid administrative credentials, our security posture becomes weaker. In order to defeat internal threats and threats that have acquired valid credentials or authentication capability, we must segment controls so that no single authority can bypass all controls. In order to accomplish this, we may need to split controls between application teams and administrators, or split access for administration between multiple administrators, as with &lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamir%27s_secret_sharing&quot;&gt;Shamir&amp;#39;s Secret Sharing (SSS)&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L226" data-git-line="226">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="226"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 226"
          title="Copy permalink for line 226"
        >226</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Threat Modelling and Threat Intelligence&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L227" data-git-line="227">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="227"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 227"
          title="Copy permalink for line 227"
        >227</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Record-keeping of all assets within an organization:&lt;/strong&gt; inventory&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L228" data-git-line="228">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="228"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 228"
          title="Copy permalink for line 228"
        >228</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;After we&amp;#39;ve completed an inventory for both systems and software and we understand our organization&amp;#39;s requirements, we&amp;#39;re ready to begin researching potential threats. Security teams research (or leverage vendor research about) threats to different industries and software. We can use this information in our &lt;a href=&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/threat_modeling&quot;&gt;Threat Modelling&lt;/a&gt;. Threat modelling describes taking data from real-world adversaries and evaluating those attack patterns and techniques against our people, processes, systems, and software. It is important to consider how the compromise of one system in our network might impact others.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L229" data-git-line="229">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/threat_intelligence&quot;&gt;Threat Intelligence&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L230" data-git-line="230">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="230"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 230"
          title="Copy permalink for line 230"
        >230</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Table-Top Tactics&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L231" data-git-line="231">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="231"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 231"
          title="Copy permalink for line 231"
        >231</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;After concerning threat intelligence or other important information is received, enterprises may benefit from immediately scheduling a cross organization discussion. One type of discussion is known as a table-top, which brings together engineers, stakeholders, and security professionals to discuss how the organization might react to various types of disasters and attacks. Conducting regular table-tops to evaluate different systems and environments is a great way to ensure that all teams know the &lt;a href=&quot;https://csrc.nist.gov/glossary/term/tactics_techniques_and_procedures&quot;&gt;Tactics, Techniques, and Procedures (TTPs)&lt;/a&gt; for handling various scenarios. Often organizations don&amp;#39;t build out proper TTPs, resulting in longer incident response times.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L232" data-git-line="232">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="232"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 232"
          title="Copy permalink for line 232"
        >232</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;BCP&lt;/strong&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L233" data-git-line="233">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="233"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 233"
          title="Copy permalink for line 233"
        >233</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">Table-top security sessions are part of &lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_continuity_planning&quot;&gt;Business Continuity Planning (BCP)&lt;/a&gt;. BCP also includes many other aspects such as live drill responses to situations like ransomware and supply-chain compromise. BCP extends outside of cybersecurity emergencies to include processes and procedures for natural disasters and gun violence. Routine table-top sessions and continuous gathering of relevant intelligence provides a proactive effort for mitigating future issues as well as rehearsing tactics, processes, and procedures.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L234" data-git-line="234">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="234"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 234"
          title="Copy permalink for line 234"
        >234</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Continuous Patching and Supply Chain Validation&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L235" data-git-line="235">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="235"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 235"
          title="Copy permalink for line 235"
        >235</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;continuous automated patching&lt;/strong&gt; is accomplished by pulling the upstream source code and applying it to the lowest development environment. Next, the change is tested, and only moved to production if it is successful. We can leverage cloud provider infrastructure to more easily spin up complete replicas of environments for testing these changes.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L236" data-git-line="236">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="236"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 236"
          title="Copy permalink for line 236"
        >236</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Continuous supply chain validation&lt;/strong&gt; occurs when people and systems validate that the software and hardware received from vendors is the expected material and that it hasn&amp;#39;t been tampered with, as well as ensuring output software and materials are verifiable by customers and business partners. Continuous supply chain validation is difficult, and sometimes requires more than software checks, such as physical inspections of equipment ordered. On the software side of supply chain security, we can use deeper testing and inspection techniques to evaluate upstream data more closely. We might opt to increase the security testing duration to attempt to detect sleeper malware implanted in upstream sources. &lt;strong&gt;Sleeper malware&lt;/strong&gt; is software that is inactive while on a a system for a period of time, potentially weeks, before it starts taking action.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L237" data-git-line="237">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="237"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 237"
          title="Copy permalink for line 237"
        >237</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Utilizing a &lt;strong&gt;software bill of materials&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href=&quot;https://www.cisa.gov/sbom&quot;&gt;SBOM&lt;/a&gt; as a way to track dependencies automatically in the application build process greatly helps us evaluate supply chain tampering. If we identify the software dependencies, create an SBOM with them, and package the container and SBOM together in a cryptographically-verifiable way, then we can verify the container&amp;#39;s SBOM signature before loading it into to production.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L238" data-git-line="238">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="238"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 238"
          title="Copy permalink for line 238"
        >238</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Encryption&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L239" data-git-line="239">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="239"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 239"
          title="Copy permalink for line 239"
        >239</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;ASPECT:&lt;/strong&gt; TLS&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L240" data-git-line="240">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="240"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 240"
          title="Copy permalink for line 240"
        >240</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Identity is accomplished through which cryptographic concept?:&lt;/strong&gt; signing&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L241" data-git-line="241">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="241"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 241"
          title="Copy permalink for line 241"
        >241</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;ephemeral encryption is &lt;a href=&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_Layer_Security&quot;&gt;TLS&lt;/a&gt;, in which nobody but the server and the client of that specific interaction can decrypt the information (not even the administrators), and the decryption keys only exist in memory for a brief time before being discarded. This type of privacy is commonly used when sending secrets or Personal Identifiable Information (PII) across the wire. PII can include names, addresses, phone numbers, email addresses, SSNs, and other information that can be used to track down or spy on an individual person.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L242" data-git-line="242">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="242"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 242"
          title="Copy permalink for line 242"
        >242</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;ensure that only the minimum required persons or systems can decrypt said data. We also probably want backups that are encrypted with different keys. In general, we don&amp;#39;t want to re-use encryption keys for different uses, as each key should only have one purpose. We also should implement protocols for routinely restoring from backups to ensure that we know how, and that the process works for every component.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L243" data-git-line="243">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="243"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 243"
          title="Copy permalink for line 243"
        >243</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Logging and Chaos Testing&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L244" data-git-line="244">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="244"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 244"
          title="Copy permalink for line 244"
        >244</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=&quot;https://www.ibm.com/garage/method/practices/manage/practice_chaotic_testing/&quot;&gt;Chaos testing&lt;/a&gt; is a type of BCP or disaster recovery &lt;a href=&quot;https://www.vmware.com/Modules/glossary/content/disaster-recovery.html&quot;&gt;DR&lt;/a&gt; practice that is often handled via automation. For example, we might leverage a virtual machine that has valid administrative credentials in the production network to cause intentional disasters from within. Chaos engineering includes a variety of different approaches, such as having red teams create chaos in the organization to test how well the organization is able to handle it, scheduling programmed machine shutdowns at various intervals, or having authenticated malicious platform API commands sent in. The goal is to test controls during messy/unpredictable situations.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L245" data-git-line="245">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="245"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 245"
          title="Copy permalink for line 245"
        >245</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h5&gt;3.5 Cybersecurity Laws, Regulations, Standards, and Frameworks&lt;/h5&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L246" data-git-line="246">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="246"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 246"
          title="Copy permalink for line 246"
        >246</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;ul&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L247" data-git-line="247">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"

        >247</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Gain a broad understanding of various legal and regulatory issues surrounding cybersecurity.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L248" data-git-line="248">
        <button

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L249" data-git-line="249">
        <button

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L250" data-git-line="250">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="250"

          title="Copy permalink for line 250"
        >250</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Laws and Regulations&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L251" data-git-line="251">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="251"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 251"
          title="Copy permalink for line 251"
        >251</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;HIPAA: The Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act of 1996 (HIPAA)1 is a United States federal law regulating health care coverage and the privacy of patient health information. Included in this law was a requirement to create of a set of standards for protecting patient health information, known as Protected Health Information (PHI). The standards that regulate how PHI can be used and disclosed are established by the &lt;strong&gt;Privacy Rule&lt;/strong&gt;. This rule sets limits on what information can be shared without a patient&amp;#39;s consent and grants patients a number of additional rights over their information, such as the right to obtain a copy of their health records.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L252" data-git-line="252">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="252"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 252"
          title="Copy permalink for line 252"
        >252</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Another rule known as the &lt;strong&gt;Security Rule&lt;/strong&gt; outlines how electronic PHI (e-PHI) must be protected. It describes three classes of safeguards that must be in place: administrative (having a designated security official, a security management process, periodic assessments, etc.), physical (facility access control, device security), and technical (access control, transmission security, audit abilities, etc.). These rules also include provisions for enforcement and monetary penalties for non-compliance. Importantly, HIPAA also requires that covered entities (healthcare providers, health plans, business associates, etc.) provide notification4 in the event that a PHI breach occurs.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L253" data-git-line="253">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="253"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 253"
          title="Copy permalink for line 253"
        >253</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;FERPA: The Family Educational Rights and Privacy Act of 1974 (FERPA)5 is a United States federal law regulating the privacy of learners&amp;#39; education records. This law6 sets limits upon the disclosure and use of these records without parents&amp;#39; or learners&amp;#39; consent. Some instances where schools are permitted to disclose these records are school transfers, cases of health or safety emergency, and compliance with a judicial order.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L254" data-git-line="254">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="254"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 254"
          title="Copy permalink for line 254"
        >254</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;FERPA also grants parents and learners over the age of 18 a number of rights over this information. These rights include the right to inspect these records, the right to request modification to inaccurate or misleading records, and more. Schools that fail to comply with these laws risk losing access to federal funding.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L255" data-git-line="255">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="255"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 255"
          title="Copy permalink for line 255"
        >255</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;GLBA: The Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act (GLBA),7 enacted by the United States Congress in 1999, establishes a number of requirements that financial institutions must follow to protect consumers&amp;#39; financial information. This law requires that institutions describe how they use and share information and allow individuals to opt out in certain cases.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L256" data-git-line="256">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="256"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 256"
          title="Copy permalink for line 256"
        >256</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Like other cybersecurity laws, GLBA requires that financial institutions ensure the confidentiality and integrity of customer financial information by anticipating threats to security and taking steps to protect against unauthorized access. In addition, financial institutions must also describe the steps that they are taking to achieve this.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L257" data-git-line="257">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="257"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 257"
          title="Copy permalink for line 257"
        >257</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;GDPR: The General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR)8 is a law adopted by the European Union9 in 2016 that regulates data privacy and security. It applies to the private sector and most public sector entities that collect and process personal data. It provides individuals with a wide set of rights over their data including the well-known &amp;quot;right to be forgotten&amp;quot; and other rights related to notifications of data breaches and portability of data between providers.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L258" data-git-line="258">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="258"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 258"
          title="Copy permalink for line 258"
        >258</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;GDPR outlines a strict legal baseline for processing personal data. For example, personal data may be processed only if the data subject has given consent, to comply with legal obligations, to perform certain tasks in the public interest, or for other &amp;quot;legitimate interests&amp;quot;. For businesses that process data on a large scale or for whom data processing is a core operation, a data protection officer - who is responsible for overseeing data protection - must be appointed.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L259" data-git-line="259">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="259"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 259"
          title="Copy permalink for line 259"
        >259</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;GDPR also establishes an independent supervisory authority to audit and enforce compliance with these regulations and administer punishment for non-compliance. The fines for violating these regulations are very high: a maximum of 20 million Euros or 4% of revenue (whichever is higher), plus any additional damages that individuals may seek.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L260" data-git-line="260">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="260"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 260"
          title="Copy permalink for line 260"
        >260</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;One unique aspect of GDPR is that it applies to any entity collecting or processing data related to people in the European Union, regardless of that entity&amp;#39;s location. At the time of its adoption, it was considered the most strict data privacy law in the world and has since become a model for a number of laws and regulations enacted around the globe.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L261" data-git-line="261">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="261"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 261"
          title="Copy permalink for line 261"
        >261</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Key disclosure laws10 are laws that compel the disclosure of cryptographic keys or passwords under specific conditions. This is typically done as part of a criminal investigation when seeking evidence of a suspected crime. A number of countries have adopted key disclosure laws requiring disclosure under varying conditions. For instance, Part III of the United Kingdom&amp;#39;s Regulation of Investigatory Powers Act 2000 (RIPA)11 grants authorities the power to force suspects to disclose decryption keys or decrypt data. Failure to comply is punishable by a maximum of two years in prison or five years if a matter of national security or child indecency is involved.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L262" data-git-line="262">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="262"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 262"
          title="Copy permalink for line 262"
        >262</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;CCPA: The California Consumer Privacy Act of 2018 (CCPA)12 is a Californian law granting residents of the state certain privacy rights concerning personal information held by for-profit businesses. One of these rights is the &amp;quot;right to know&amp;quot;, which requires business to to disclose to consumers, upon request, what personal information has been collected, used, and sold about them, and why. The &amp;quot;right to opt-out&amp;quot; also allows consumers to request that their personal information not be sold, something that must, with few exceptions, be approved. Another right is the &amp;quot;right to delete&amp;quot;, which allows consumers to request that businesses delete collected personal information. In this case, however, there are a number of exceptions that allow business to decline these requests.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L263" data-git-line="263">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="263"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 263"
          title="Copy permalink for line 263"
        >263</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;1</span>
      </li>

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L287" data-git-line="287">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="287"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 287"
          title="Copy permalink for line 287"
        >287</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Standards and Frameworks&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L288" data-git-line="288">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="288"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 288"
          title="Copy permalink for line 288"
        >288</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;PCI DSS:&lt;/strong&gt; The Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard (PCI DSS)1 is an information security standard, first published in 2004, for organizations handling customer payment data for a number of major credit card companies. It is managed by the Payment Card Industry Standards Council. It&amp;#39;s purpose is to ensure that payment data is properly secured in order to reduce the risk of credit card fraud. As with other frameworks, PCI DSS consists of a number of requirements, compliance with which must be assessed annually. Most of these requirements resemble other industry best practices regarding network and system security, access control, vulnerability management, monitoring, etc. For example, Requirement 2 prohibits the use of vendor-supplied defaults for system passwords and other security-related parameters. Other requirements are credit-card specific formulations of other familiar best practices. For example, Requirement 3 outlines what types of credit card data can be stored and how it must be protected.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L289" data-git-line="289">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="289"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 289"
          title="Copy permalink for line 289"
        >289</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CIS Top 18:&lt;/strong&gt; The Center for Internet Security (CIS) Critical Security Controls, also known as CIS Controls,2 are a set of 18 (previously 20) recommended controls intended to increase an organization&amp;#39;s security posture. While not themselves laws or regulations, these controls pertain to a number of areas that regulations are concerned with, including data protection, access control management, continuous vulnerability management, malware detection, and more.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L290" data-git-line="290">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="290"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 290"
          title="Copy permalink for line 290"
        >290</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;These controls are divided into a number of safeguards (previously known as sub-controls), which, in turn, are grouped into three implementation groups intended to help prioritize safeguard implementation. IG1 consists of controls that are considered the minimum standard for information security meant to protect against the most common attacks and should be implemented by every organization. They are typically implemented by small businesses with limited IT expertise that manage data of low sensitivity. IG2 is composed of additional safeguards that are meant to apply to more complex organizations, typically those with multiple departments and staff dedicated to managing IT infrastructure with more sensitive customer and proprietary data. IG3, which consists of all safeguards, is typically implemented by organizations with dedicated cybersecurity experts managing sensitive data that may be subject to oversight.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L291" data-git-line="291">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="291"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 291"
          title="Copy permalink for line 291"
        >291</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;NIST Cybersecurity Framework:&lt;/strong&gt; The National Institute for Standards and Technology (NIST) Cybersecurity Framework4 is a collection of standards and practices designed to help organizations understand and reduce cybersecurity risk. It was originally developed to help protect critical infrastructure; however, it has been subsequently adopted by a wide array of organizations.5&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L292" data-git-line="292">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="292"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 292"
          title="Copy permalink for line 292"
        >292</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The NIST framework consists of three components:6 Core, Implementation Tiers, and Profiles. The Framework Core is a set of cybersecurity activities and outcomes. It is divided into five high-level functions that encompass a number of categories (for example, Asset Management and Risk Assessment). These categories, in turn, include subcategories that consist of statements describing the outcome of improved security and which are aligned with Information References. These references go into deeper detail about possible technical implementations. For example, Subcategory ID.BE-1 (Function: Identify, Category: Business Environment) states &amp;quot;The organization&amp;#39;s role in the supply chain is identified and communicated.&amp;quot;&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L293" data-git-line="293">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="293"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 293"
          title="Copy permalink for line 293"
        >293</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The Framework Implementation Tiers specify the degree to which an organization&amp;#39;s Cybersecurity practices satisfy the outcome described by the subcategories of the Framework Core. There are four such Tiers: partial (the least degree), risk informed, repeatable, and adaptive. Framework Profiles refer to the relationship between the present implementation of an organization&amp;#39;s cybersecurity activities (Current Profile) and their desired outcome (Target Profile), which is determined by the organization&amp;#39;s business objectives, requirements, controls and risk appetite. The comparison of these profiles can help the organization perform a gap analysis, as well as understand and prioritize the work required to fill it.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L294" data-git-line="294">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="294"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 294"
          title="Copy permalink for line 294"
        >294</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;ATT3CK and D3FEND:&lt;/strong&gt; The MITRE organization has tabulated and organized a framework for cataloging how groups of attackers work together to infiltrate systems and achieve their goals. This framework, called the MITRE ATT3CK8 framework, is constantly updated to reflect the latest TTPs used by malicious groups across the globe. More details about the ATT3CK framework and how adversaries can be classified is available in OffSec&amp;#39;s SOC-200 course.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L295" data-git-line="295">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="295"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 295"
          title="Copy permalink for line 295"
        >295</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;More recently, MITRE released a mirrored framework from the defensive perspective. While ATT3CK is meant to catalog and categorize the various ways that threat actors operate in the real world, D3FEND9 portrays a set of best practices, actions, and methodologies employed by defenders to prevent, detect, mitigate, and react to attacks.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L296" data-git-line="296">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="296"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 296"
          title="Copy permalink for line 296"
        >296</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cyber Kill Chain:&lt;/strong&gt; The Cyber Kill Chain is a methodology developed by Lockheed Martin to help defenders identify and defend against cyber attacks. It outlines seven stages of the attack lifecycle: reconnaissance, weaponization, delivery, exploitation, installation, command and control, and actions on objectives.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L297" data-git-line="297">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="297"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 297"
          title="Copy permalink for line 297"
        >297</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;In the reconnaissance phase, an attacker identifies a target and enumerates potential weaknesses through which it may be exploited. Weaponization is the process by which an attack method to exploit this weakness is identified. This attack is launched in the delivery phase and, in the exploitation phase, the payload is executed on the target system. This leads to the installation stage in which malware is installed on the system. This malware is used to execute further commands in the command and control phase. In the actions on objectives phase, the attacker performs the actions required to achieve their ultimate goals, which may be data theft, modification, destruction, etc.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L298" data-git-line="298">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="298"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 298"
          title="Copy permalink for line 298"
        >298</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;FedRAMP:&lt;/strong&gt; The Federal Risk and Authorization Management Program (FedRAMP)12 is a United States program13 that provides a standardized security framework for cloud services used by the federal government. Whereas previously, a cloud service may have been required to obtain different authorizations for different federal agencies, FedRAMP allows a cloud service to obtain a single authorization for all government agencies. Its goal is to accelerate the government&amp;#39;s adoption of cloud services while also ensuring that these services are secure. The controls are based off of NIST SP 800-53 Revision 4 and enhanced by a number of additional controls that pertain specifically to cloud computing. More details pertaining to cloud technology are explored in OffSec&amp;#39;s CLD-100.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L299" data-git-line="299">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="299"

          title="Copy permalink for line 299"
        >299</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;1</span>
      </li>

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L325" data-git-line="325">
        <button
