          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 325"
          title="Copy permalink for line 325"
        >325</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h5&gt;3.6 Career Opportunities in Cybersecurity&lt;/h5&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L326" data-git-line="326">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="326"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 326"
          title="Copy permalink for line 326"
        >326</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Cybersecurity Career Opportunities: Attack&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L327" data-git-line="327">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="327"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 327"
          title="Copy permalink for line 327"
        >327</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Network Penetration Tester: A Network Penetration Tester is responsible for discovering and exploiting vulnerabilities that exist in a targeted network. This career may be a good choice for someone who has a strong understanding of networking and systems and enjoys finding ways of subverting their security measures. This role also benefits from clear technical writing abilities. To learn such skills, we suggest reviewing OffSec&amp;#39;s PEN courses at the 100, 200, and 300 levels.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L328" data-git-line="328">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="328"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 328"
          title="Copy permalink for line 328"
        >328</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Web Application Testers: A Web Application Tester is responsible for testing web applications for security weaknesses. A good candidate for this role likely has a strong knowledge of web application vulnerabilities, enjoys testing them, and enjoys subverting the security measures that they employ. The skills required to become a Web Application Tester are covered in the WEB track at the 100, 200, and 300 levels. These Modules teach the basics of how web applications work as well black-box and white-box approaches to web application testing.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L329" data-git-line="329">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="329"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 329"
          title="Copy permalink for line 329"
        >329</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Cloud Penetration Tester: A Cloud Penetration Tester is responsible for performing penetration testing on cloud infrastructure. This might be a good career path for someone who has knowledge and experience in cloud infrastructure and penetration testing. As with other penetration testing positions, you may enjoy this role if you have fun probing infrastructure for weaknesses and figuring out ways to exploit them. CLD-100 teaches learners how to test, attack, and exploit cloud technologies.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L330" data-git-line="330">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="330"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 330"
          title="Copy permalink for line 330"
        >330</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Exploit Developer: An Exploit Developer is responsible for discovering and developing exploits for software vulnerabilities. Someone looking to become an Exploit Developer might enjoy reverse engineering applications to determine how they work, reading low-level code, and bypassing security mitigations. The EXP-301 course offers more information about Windows binary exploitation, while EXP-312 explores macOS logical exploitation.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L331" data-git-line="331">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="331"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 331"
          title="Copy permalink for line 331"
        >331</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Vulnerability Researcher:A Vulnerability Researcher is responsible for researching new software vulnerabilities and exploitation techniques, determining their impact, developing Proofs of Concept (PoCs), and communicating their findings to different stakeholders. A person may wish to be a Vulnerability Researcher if they enjoy reverse engineering and researching new and emerging vulnerabilities and techniques. You can follow EXP-301 and EXP-312 to learn how to reverse engineer and develop exploits for Windows and macOS software, respectively.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L332" data-git-line="332">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="332"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 332"
          title="Copy permalink for line 332"
        >332</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Cybersecurity Career Opportunities: Defend&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L333" data-git-line="333">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="333"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 333"
          title="Copy permalink for line 333"
        >333</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;SOC Analyst: A SOC Analyst is responsible for monitoring, triaging and, when necessary, escalating security alerts that arise from within monitored networks. Someone may be a good fit for this position if they enjoy investigating and gathering information surrounding suspicious activity. To prepare, we recommend following the SOC track at the 100 and 200 levels in the OffSec library. SOC Modules will explore the techniques attackers use to infiltrate networks and those that analysts use to discover this activity.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L334" data-git-line="334">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="334"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 334"
          title="Copy permalink for line 334"
        >334</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Malware Analyst: A Malware Analyst is responsible for analyzing suspected or confirmed malware samples in order to determine how they work and, ultimately, what their purpose is. Someone might enjoy this role if they have a basic understanding of networking and like analyzing suspicious samples and reverse engineering.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L335" data-git-line="335">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="335"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 335"
          title="Copy permalink for line 335"
        >335</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The OffSec library contains a number of resources that can help learners learn these skills. For example, EXP-301 teaches reverse engineering and some basics of the Windows API. PEN courses at the 200 and 300 levels describe how attackers craft malicious documents and payloads as well as the techniques that they use to evade antivirus and other detection mechanisms. Finally, the 100-level library contains Modules that can help to learn the basics of networking.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L336" data-git-line="336">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="336"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 336"
          title="Copy permalink for line 336"
        >336</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Digital Forensics Analyst: A Digital Forensics Analyst is responsible for investigating Cybersecurity incidents by gathering and analyzing evidence of intrusions and recovering data. Someone who enjoys this role likely has a strong understanding of how systems and networks operate and is interested in investigating how intrusions occur, then assembling evidence into a complete story. To begin learning these skills, we recommend reviewing the SOC track at the 100 and 200 levels. SOC-200 shows some of the specific ways attackers operate and how to search for evidence of their attacks.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L337" data-git-line="337">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="337"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 337"
          title="Copy permalink for line 337"
        >337</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Incident Responder: An Incident Responder is responsible for reacting to cybersecurity events. This includes identifying the cause and scope of an incident and recommending measures to contain, eliminate, and recover from it. Someone may be a good fit for this role if they have a strong technical background and enjoy working in a fast-paced environment and performing root cause analysis. This role also benefits from strong cross-functional communication skills. Starting with the SOC track at the 100 and 200 level will help learners prepare for this career. SOC-200 in particular shows some of the ways attackers operate and how to search for evidence of their attacks.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L338" data-git-line="338">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"

          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 338"

        >338</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Threat Hunter: A Threat Hunter is responsible for proactively searching networks and systems for Indicators of Compromise (IOCs) using the most up-to-date threat intelligence. This role could be a good choice for someone who enjoys following the most recent cybersecurity feeds and searching for malicious activity that may have evaded existing defenses. There are a number of resources in the OffSec library that can help to prepare for this position. For example, the SOC track at the 100 and 200 levels teaches about common techniques used by attackers and how to search for and identify them. The PEN-300 course is helpful to learn about the ways that attackers bypass existing defenses.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L339" data-git-line="339">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Cybersecurity Career Opportunities: Build&lt;/h6&gt;</span>

This role encompasses a number of more specialized positions, including Cloud Architect, and, with the usual exception of that position, typically involves the implementation of the cloud architecture as outlined by the company&amp;#39;s cloud-computing strategy. This career may be a good fit for someone who enjoys programming and building infrastructure, and has experience with cloud service providers and other cloud-related technologies.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>

Individuals with a deep, cutting-edge understanding of cloud computing who enjoy developing high-level business strategy and excel at communicating technical concepts across business areas may enjoy this role.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L342" data-git-line="342">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Developer: A Software Developer is responsible for writing computer programs which, depending on the precise role, may range from core operating system components to desktop, mobile and web applications. Someone who enjoys designing elegant and efficient programmatic solutions to problems may enjoy this role. Depending on the type of software development, the OffSec Library contains a considerable number of resources to help learners understand attack vectors and create secure software. A general understanding of software vulnerabilities is available in the PEN-200 course, while information about web development can be found in OffSec&amp;#39;s WEB courses at the 200 and 300 level. Those who may be programming in memory-unsafe languages such as C may be interested in the EXP-301 and EXP-312 courses.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>

A DevSecOps Engineer5 is responsible for automating security testing and other security-related processes. This role might be a good fit for someone who has an understanding of Continuous Integration / Continuous Development (CI/CD) pipeline and tools, an interest in security testing automation, and the ability to work in a fast-paced environment.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L345" data-git-line="345">

This information can be found in the WEB and PEN courses at the 200 and 300 level. CLD-100 also provides details about Docker and Kubernetes: two essential tools for DevSecOps.&lt;/p&gt;</span>

A person may wish to be a Site Reliability Engineer if they have software development experience and are interested in using automation to monitor for, alert, and respond to reliability-related issues. learners can learn about containers and Kubernetes, some of the key technologies used to support SRE, by following CLD-100 in the OffSec library.&lt;/p&gt;</span>

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;System Hardener (System Administrator): A System Hardener is responsible for configuring systems to reduce their security risk. This involves changing insecure default configurations, removing unused programs, ensuring firewalls are appropriately restrictive, etc. A person may seek out this career if they have experience with system administration, are familiar with attack techniques, and enjoy making systems and the data they store more secure. Many of the skills required for this position are covered in the PEN track at the 100, 200 and 300 levels. PEN-100, for instance, explores some of the basics of networking and system administration. PEN-200 describes some of the common techniques that attackers use. PEN-300 teaches more advanced techniques that attackers use to bypass defenses.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L365" data-git-line="365">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="365"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 365"
          title="Copy permalink for line 365"
        >365</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;The Forgetting Curve and Cognitive Load&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L366" data-git-line="366">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="366"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 366"
          title="Copy permalink for line 366"
        >366</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;In 1885, learning scientist Hermann Ebbinghaus set out to memorize a few documents, then tested himself repeatedly on what he remembered. He was only able to remember all of the details if he tested himself immediately after memorizing. Ebbinghaus found he only remembered 100 percent of the information at the time of acquisition. After that, he started forgetting information very quickly. When he waited 20 minutes, he could only remember 58%. A day later, he could only remember 23%. He called this decline The &lt;strong&gt;Forgetting Curve&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L367" data-git-line="367">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="367"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 367"
          title="Copy permalink for line 367"
        >367</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The second problem, which we&amp;#39;ve referred to as &amp;quot;too much information at once&amp;quot;, is usually referred to as &lt;strong&gt;Cognitive Load&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L368" data-git-line="368">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="368"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 368"
          title="Copy permalink for line 368"
        >368</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;To better understand cognitive load, it may be helpful to imagine our brain as sort of a room, with pieces of information (that take up space) moving in and out. At some point, if more and more information keeps coming in, there simply isn&amp;#39;t enough space for everything to stay organized. Pretty soon, the room is too full and there isn&amp;#39;t enough space for more to come in through the door.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L369" data-git-line="369">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="369"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 369"
          title="Copy permalink for line 369"
        >369</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h5&gt;4.2 Unique Challenges to Learning Technical Skills&lt;/h5&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L370" data-git-line="370">

        >370</button>

        <button

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L375" data-git-line="375">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="375"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 375"
          title="Copy permalink for line 375"
        >375</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Digital vs. Print Materials&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L376" data-git-line="376">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="376"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 376"
          title="Copy permalink for line 376"
        >376</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Perhaps the more important thing to note here, has to do with a concept known as Contextual Learning. Although we can&amp;#39;t explore all of its details in this Module, this concept suggests that even on an intuitive level, we know that it&amp;#39;s easier to learn how to build a house on a construction site.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L377" data-git-line="377">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="377"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 377"
          title="Copy permalink for line 377"
        >377</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;In other words, when the training material is presented in the same context as the skill that we&amp;#39;re trying to learn, our brain has to do less translation work and can accept the new information more readily. This doesn&amp;#39;t mean that books about computers are worthless - it just means that our brains have to do more work to assimilate information from the page and think about it in the context of the computer screen.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L378" data-git-line="378">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="378"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 378"
          title="Copy permalink for line 378"
        >378</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Expecting the Unexpected&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L379" data-git-line="379">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="379"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 379"
          title="Copy permalink for line 379"
        >379</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;learning about cyber security is similar to learning &lt;a href=&quot;https://ervet-journal.springeropen.com/articles/10.1186/s40461-020-00100-0&quot;&gt;transversal skills&lt;/a&gt; like leadership, communication, and teamwork. As with these skills, we cannot afford to focus on memorizing a series of steps to take. There is no simple, straightforward standard operating procedure for building better teamwork just as there is no simple, straightforward standard operating procedure for exploit development. Instead, we need to focus on understanding methods, techniques, and the purpose behind certain actions.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L380" data-git-line="380">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="380"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 380"
          title="Copy permalink for line 380"
        >380</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Let&amp;#39;s return briefly to our example of learning how to secure a network. We mentioned that &amp;quot;A network that is secure today may not be secure in six months.&amp;quot; The best approach to this problem is not to learn a series of steps we can follow to make that network secure today, then learn a new set of steps in six months. The solution is to learn the methodology and the purpose behind each security step. When new risks arise, we&amp;#39;ll apply the same methodology, adapting and evolving along with the changing threat landscape.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L381" data-git-line="381">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="381"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 381"
          title="Copy permalink for line 381"
        >381</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;The Challenges of Remote and Asynchronous Learning&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L382" data-git-line="382">
        <button

        <button

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=&quot;https://www.studytonight.com/linux-guide/understanding-file-permissions-in-linux-unix&quot;&gt;file permissions&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;</span>

        >424</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Let&amp;#39;s open a terminal and review how this works in practice. We&amp;#39;ll &lt;code&gt;touch&lt;/code&gt; a file (newfilename.txt), which will create it and automatically make us the owner. Then we&amp;#39;ll use the listing command ls3 to gather information about the file, providing the -l parameter that will produce a long listing including the file permissions.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L425" data-git-line="425">

        <button

          class="git-code-anchor"

          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 428"
          title="Copy permalink for line 428"
        >428</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The output of the ls command includes information about the permissions as indicated by the letters rwx, where the &amp;quot;r&amp;quot; is for read, the &amp;quot;w&amp;quot; is for write, and the &amp;quot;x&amp;quot; is for execute. A dash (-) indicates that the user class doesn&amp;#39;t have the corresponding permissions. In this case, we have permission to read and write to our new file, but there is no &amp;quot;x&amp;quot; character in the output, meaning no class has permission to execute.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L429" data-git-line="429">

      </li>

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Let&amp;#39;s change the executable permission for this file and give ourselves permission to execute the file (put another way, to run it as a program).       </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L434" data-git-line="434">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Since the only thing that seems to be &amp;quot;broken&amp;quot; with our chmod file is the permissions (as far as we know, the contents of the file itself are fine), let&amp;#39;s try to copy only the contents of the file and not the permissions. Instead of just sending the contents of the file to display in the terminal window, we can use the &amp;quot;&amp;gt;&amp;quot; character to send them into our chmodfix file.&lt;/p&gt;</span>

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;We previously examined the -rwxr-xr-x portion of the output. After we run the cat command, we&amp;#39;ll observe that the file name and the permissions are still the same as before, but the file size is now &amp;quot;64448&amp;quot;.       <li class="git-code-line" id="L488" data-git-line="488">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Let&amp;#39;s go one step further and restore our system so that we don&amp;#39;t run into this problem again. Let&amp;#39;s try and run the chmodfix command on the original chmod file to fix things.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L489" data-git-line="489">
        <button

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L490" data-git-line="490">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The first step is to divide the page into three areas. These are the cue (on the left hand side of the page), the notes (the large area on the right hand side of the page), and the summary (several lines of space at the bottom of the page).&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L513" data-git-line="513">
        <button
          type="button"

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The cue might be questions we have about the text or key words or phrases. To illustrate an example, let&amp;#39;s discuss a Module like password hashing. This Module might have key terms to learn such as one-way encryption, salting, and cracking passwords. We might also have a question, for example, &amp;quot;Are some hashing methods better than others?&amp;quot;&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L514" data-git-line="514">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The notes section for that page should be directly related to the items in the cue section. For example, near where we&amp;#39;ve written one-way encryption, we might write a long form definition of what is meant by this term.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L515" data-git-line="515">

Interested in more about cracking.&amp;quot; The content here does not need to necessarily be directly related to the material&lt;/p&gt;</span>

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L521" data-git-line="521">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="521"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 521"
          title="Copy permalink for line 521"
        >521</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Spaced practice has to do with the timing and duration of our study time. It is recommended to spread out the study time over days and weeks rather than do it all at once. Long, &amp;quot;cramming&amp;quot;-style study sessions actually take more time, often come at the expense of sleep, and (because they overwhelm our cognitive load) are significantly less effective.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L522" data-git-line="522">
        <button
          type="button"

          data-git-line-anchor="522"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 522"

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;The SQ3R Method&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L523" data-git-line="523">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="523"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 523"
          title="Copy permalink for line 523"
        >523</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href=&quot;https://ucc.vt.edu/academic_support/study_skills_information/sq3r_reading-study_system.html&quot;&gt;SQ3R method&lt;/a&gt; has learners follow a pattern of study activities - survey, question, read, recite, review.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L524" data-git-line="524">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="524"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 524"
          title="Copy permalink for line 524"
        >524</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;In the case of our current Module, a learner might encounter the various headings and subheadings: Learning Theory, Unique Challenges to Learning Technical Skills, Offsec Training Methodology, and so on. They might then review the subheadings.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L525" data-git-line="525">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="525"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 525"
          title="Copy permalink for line 525"
        >525</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Next, they will create, preferably in writing, a list of questions that they hope to have answered via the material. This may or may not reflect what the material will actually cover, but should be based largely on the survey. This is a very important step, as learners will return to the questions repeatedly.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L526" data-git-line="526">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"

          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 526"

        >526</button>

      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L527" data-git-line="527">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="527"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 527"
          title="Copy permalink for line 527"
        >527</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Next, the learner returns to their list of questions for that smaller section. They should try and recite the questions back from memory and determine if they&amp;#39;re now able to answer them.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L528" data-git-line="528">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="528"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 528"
          title="Copy permalink for line 528"
        >528</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Finally, in the review, a learner returns to all of the smaller sections from a larger Module or chapter to check whether or not the questions have been answered and they can recall the answers.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;It&amp;#39;s entirely possible that a few weeks into this plan you will need to adjust it or change it, and that&amp;#39;s fine. In fact, the best plans often need to be adjusted over time. The alternative - having no plan at all - would mean studying in an ad hoc manner, picking up (and putting down) materials whenever convenient.&lt;/p&gt;</span>

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Use Time Allotment Strategies&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L570" data-git-line="570">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="570"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 570"
          title="Copy permalink for line 570"
        >570</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Spaced Practice, requires looking at a calendar, finding reasonable time slots, and sticking to a schedule. In addition to avoiding &amp;quot;marathon&amp;quot; sessions whenever possible, there are a few things to consider when choosing the best times to schedule studying.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L571" data-git-line="571">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="571"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 571"
          title="Copy permalink for line 571"
        >571</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Some research2has suggested that before sleep might be a great time to study. The danger here is that it is quite easy to push back the bedtime to continue studying. Intuitively, we might think that we are being more productive by staying up later and studying more, but a lack of sleep can negatively impact our brain&amp;#39;s ability to retain information. Planning study time also means planning an end to the studying.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L572" data-git-line="572">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="572"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 572"
          title="Copy permalink for line 572"
        >572</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;If sleep is important for studying, a learner might correctly assume that exercise is as well. Intense physical activity increases blood to flow to the brain, and fires neurons in the hippocampus (the center for memory). In addition to generally improving brain health, exercising either before or after studying can be highly beneficial to improving memory and recall.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L573" data-git-line="573">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;While the general execution plan for a penetration test will often follow a particular model, most pentests tend to follow the maxim &amp;quot;no plan survives first contact with the enemy&amp;quot;&lt;/p&gt;</span>

        >613</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;The General Structure of Penetration Testing Notes&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L614" data-git-line="614">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="614"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 614"
          title="Copy permalink for line 614"
        >614</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Notes must be concise and precise&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L615" data-git-line="615">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="615"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 615"
          title="Copy permalink for line 615"
        >615</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;ul&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L616" data-git-line="616">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="616"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 616"
          title="Copy permalink for line 616"
        >616</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Rather than taking a few general notes assuming that we&amp;#39;ll remember how to perform certain actions next time, we should record exactly what we did.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L617" data-git-line="617">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="617"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 617"
          title="Copy permalink for line 617"
        >617</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;This means that every command that we type, every line of code that we modify, and even anywhere we click in the GUI should be recorded so that we can reproduce our actions.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L618" data-git-line="618">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="618"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 618"
          title="Copy permalink for line 618"
        >618</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Even if we&amp;#39;ve taken a lot of notes, if looking at them later doesn&amp;#39;t help us remember exactly what happened during the assessment, then they won&amp;#39;t be particularly useful to us.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L619" data-git-line="619">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="619"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 619"
          title="Copy permalink for line 619"
        >619</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;The notes need to be structured and sufficiently detailed to remove any ambiguity.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L620" data-git-line="620">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="620"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 620"
          title="Copy permalink for line 620"
        >620</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;To write a convincing and substantiated technical report later, we need to provide sufficient technical details within our notes.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L621" data-git-line="621">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="621"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 621"
          title="Copy permalink for line 621"
        >621</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;If the notes are not written coherently, it will be difficult for someone else to repeat the test and get the same results.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L622" data-git-line="622">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="622"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 622"
          title="Copy permalink for line 622"
        >622</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;/ul&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L623" data-git-line="623">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="623"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 623"
          title="Copy permalink for line 623"
        >623</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;A note-taking structure that starts broad and drills down into each section is an easy and expandable method of taking notes. The top-down approach guides us to start with the broadest activity, and then narrow down our focus and expand the level of detail until we have everything we need to replicate exactly what happened.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L624" data-git-line="624">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="624"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 624"
          title="Copy permalink for line 624"
        >624</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Let&amp;#39;s now look at an example of the notes we might take for a web vulnerability we discovered:&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L625" data-git-line="625">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="625"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 625"

        >625</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Must Include Sufficient Detail Points for the Issue&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L626" data-git-line="626">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L627" data-git-line="627">
        <button

          class="git-code-anchor"

        >627</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Application Name:&lt;/strong&gt; This is important in a multi-application test, and a good habit to get into. The application names also lends itself to building a natural folder and file structure quite nicely.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L628" data-git-line="628">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="628"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 628"
          title="Copy permalink for line 628"
        >628</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;URL:&lt;/strong&gt; This is the exact URL that would be used to locate the vulnerability that we&amp;#39;ve detected.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L629" data-git-line="629">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="629"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 629"
          title="Copy permalink for line 629"
        >629</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Request Type:&lt;/strong&gt; This represents both the type of request (i.e: GET, POST, OPTIONS, etc) that was made, as well as any manual changes we made to it. For example, we might intercept a POST request message and change the username or password before forwarding it on.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L630" data-git-line="630">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="630"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 630"
          title="Copy permalink for line 630"
        >630</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Issue Detail:&lt;/strong&gt; This is the overview of the vulnerability that will be triggered by our actions. For example, we may point to a CVE describing the vulnerability if one exists, and/or explain the impact we observe. We may categorize the impact as denial of service, remote code execution, privilege escalation, and so on.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L631" data-git-line="631">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="631"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 631"
          title="Copy permalink for line 631"
        >631</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Proof of Concept Payload:&lt;/strong&gt; This is a string or code block that will trigger the vulnerability. This is the most important part of the note, as it is what will drive the issue home and allow it to be replicated. It should list all of the necessary preconditions, and provide the exact code or commands that would need to be used to perform the triggers the vulnerability again.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>

        <button

        >670</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Taking Screenshots&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L671" data-git-line="671">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="671"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 671"
          title="Copy permalink for line 671"
        >671</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Screenshots are an important way to communicate the visual impact of a finding, and can be far more effective than mere text. For example, it&amp;#39;s more effective to show a screenshot of an alert box popping up from an XSS payload than to describe it in words. However, it&amp;#39;s more difficult to use a screenshot to describe exactly what&amp;#39;s happening when we use something like a buffer overflow payload. Just like we want to use the right tool to perform certain attacks, so we also want to use the right tool to show certain results (such as text vs images).&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L672" data-git-line="672">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="672"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 672"
          title="Copy permalink for line 672"
        >672</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;We can use screenshots to supplement our note-taking or to include them in our report to illustrate the steps we took, which will help another tester reproduce the issues. However, we need to be conscious of the audience. While a penetration tester may consider an alert window to demonstrate XSS as perfectly self-explanatory, developers unfamiliar with the vulnerability may not understand its true cause or impact. It&amp;#39;s good practice to always support a screenshot with text.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L673" data-git-line="673">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="673"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 673"
          title="Copy permalink for line 673"
        >673</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Screenshots have a specific goal, which is to convey information that would take several sentences to describe or to make an impact. With this in mind, the screenshot should contain exactly enough information to justify not using text, but there shouldn&amp;#39;t be too much information to make the screenshot confusing.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L674" data-git-line="674">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="674"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 674"
          title="Copy permalink for line 674"
        >674</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;There are several pitfalls we should avoid when using screenshots. We have already discussed making sure the screenshots are legible. We must also ensure there isn&amp;#39;t more than one concept illustrated in each screenshot. A screenshot that contains two pieces of pertinent information does not lend itself to being easily understood at a glance. We must also ensure the impact is framed properly in the screenshot.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L675" data-git-line="675">

          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 699"
          title="Copy permalink for line 699"
        >699</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Recognize when to use appendices, resources, and references.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L700" data-git-line="700">

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;/ul&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L701" data-git-line="701">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="701"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 701"
          title="Copy permalink for line 701"
        >701</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Purpose of a Technical Report&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L702" data-git-line="702">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="702"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 702"
          title="Copy permalink for line 702"
        >702</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;To properly prepare a report for the client, we must understand two things:&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L703" data-git-line="703">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="703"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 703"
          title="Copy permalink for line 703"
        >703</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;ol&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L704" data-git-line="704">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="704"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 704"
          title="Copy permalink for line 704"
        >704</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;The purpose of the report.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L705" data-git-line="705">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="705"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 705"
          title="Copy permalink for line 705"
        >705</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;How we can deliver the information we&amp;#39;ve collected in a way that the audience can understand.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L706" data-git-line="706">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="706"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 706"
          title="Copy permalink for line 706"
        >706</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;/ol&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L707" data-git-line="707">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="707"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 707"
          title="Copy permalink for line 707"
        >707</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The end goal is for the client to be presented with a path forward that outlines and highlights all the flaws that are currently present in their systems within the scope of the engagement, ways to fix those flaws in an immediate sense, and strategic goals that will prevent those vulnerabilities from appearing in the future. This output is often provided in the form of a penetration testing report. As far as the client is concerned, the report is (usually) the only deliverable of the engagement that truly matters.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L708" data-git-line="708">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="708"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 708"
          title="Copy permalink for line 708"
        >708</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;In many cases where we don&amp;#39;t find vulnerabilities, we should avoid including too many technical details on what we did in the report. A simple statement that no vulnerabilities have been found is often sufficient. &lt;strong&gt;We should ensure that we don&amp;#39;t confuse the client with the technical details of our attempts&lt;/strong&gt;, as this will undermine the value of the issues we did actually find It&amp;#39;s the tester’s job to present that information in a way that is easy to understand and act upon. &lt;strong&gt;That said, some clients may prefer verbose and deep technical reports even on non-issues&lt;/strong&gt;, which leads to another &lt;strong&gt;consideration: the audience&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L709" data-git-line="709">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="709"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 709"
          title="Copy permalink for line 709"
        >709</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Must have an understanding client key business goals and objectives. This is another reason why being clear on the Rules of Engagement is so important, because it gives us a window into the client&amp;#39;s core concerns.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L710" data-git-line="710">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="710"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 710"
          title="Copy permalink for line 710"
        >710</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;All issues discovered in the course of testing should be documented but we will want to highlight any issues we find that would affect these key areas. Examples of client-specific key areas of concern could include HIPAA,1 which is a framework that governs medical data in the US, and PCI,2 which is a framework that governs credit card and payment processing.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L711" data-git-line="711">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="711"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 711"
          title="Copy permalink for line 711"
        >711</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Client A/B Example:&lt;/strong&gt; Assume that Client A is a hospital and Client B is a bank.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L712" data-git-line="712">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="712"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 712"
          title="Copy permalink for line 712"
        >712</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Client A is a hospital with medical devices connected to their network, doctors and patients who need action to be taken quickly in response to monitoring alerts are very likely to be worried about network up-time and machine readiness. Medical devices connected to the network are often running on old machines with obsolete versions of embedded software. The need for continuous operations may have resulted in these devices missing upgrades and patches. While reporting, the vulnerabilities we find should be highlighted, and then we might make a suggestion to isolate the machines on their own logical subnet given that upgrades or patching cannot be applied promptly.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L713" data-git-line="713">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="713"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 713"
          title="Copy permalink for line 713"
        >713</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;On the other hand, this exact same scenario on Client B’s network could be catastrophic. If a server or device in a bank is missing a patch, that could very well be a foothold into the network. Because systems will need to communicate with other systems on the network, complete segmentation may not be feasible. Therefore, a missing patch is of far greater concern and may need to be reported as a critical issue.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L714" data-git-line="714">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="714"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 714"
          title="Copy permalink for line 714"
        >714</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Keep in mind the &lt;strong&gt;situation under which the vulnerability may be exploited and its potential impact&lt;/strong&gt;. A clear text HTTP login on the internet is considered extremely unsafe. On an internal network, while still unsafe, it is less concerning given that more steps must be accomplished to properly exploit it. In much the same way, a hospital may not care that their Internet-facing login portal accepts TLS 1.0 ciphers. An eCommerce site is likely to be much more concerned, given the PCI violation that accepting TLS 1.0 creates.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L715" data-git-line="715">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="715"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 715"
          title="Copy permalink for line 715"
        >715</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Tailor the Content&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L716" data-git-line="716">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="716"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 716"
          title="Copy permalink for line 716"
        >716</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;We must deliver skill-appropriate content for all the readers of our report. It may be read by executives, the heads of security, and by technical members of the security team. This means we want to not only provide a simple overview of the issues for the executives, but we will also want to provide sufficient technical detail for the more technical readers.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L717" data-git-line="717">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="717"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 717"
          title="Copy permalink for line 717"
        >717</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;h6&gt;Executive Summary&lt;/h6&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L718" data-git-line="718">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="718"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 718"
          title="Copy permalink for line 718"
        >718</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The first section of the report should be an Executive Summary. This enables senior management to understand the scope and outcomes of the testing at a sufficient level to understand the value of the test, and to approve remediation. We start with the quick bite-sized pieces of information that provide the big picture, and follow that up with the full Executive Summary.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L719" data-git-line="719">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="719"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 719"
          title="Copy permalink for line 719"
        >719</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;The Executive Summary should start with outlining the scope of the engagement. Having a clear scope agreed upon in advance of the testing defines the bounds of what will be covered. We then want to be very clear as to what exactly was tested and whether anything was dropped from the scope. Timing issues, such as insufficient testing time due to finding too many vulnerabilities to adequately report on, should be included to ensure that the scope statement for any subsequent test is appropriate. Including the scope statement in the report protects the penetration tester from any suggestion of not having completed the required testing. It also gives the client a more accurate model of what is practical given the budget and time constraints that were initially set.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L720" data-git-line="720">

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L741" data-git-line="741">
        <button

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L742" data-git-line="742">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="742"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 742"
          title="Copy permalink for line 742"
        >742</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;Next, we&amp;#39;ll prepare the long-form Executive Summary. This is a written summary of the testing that provides a high-level overview of each step of the engagement and establishes severity, context, and a &amp;quot;worst-case scenario&amp;quot; for the key findings from the testing. It&amp;#39;s important not to undersell or oversell the vulnerabilities. We want the client&amp;#39;s mental model of their security posture to be accurate. For example, if we&amp;#39;ve found an SQL injection that enables credit card details to be stolen, then that represents a very different severity than if we&amp;#39;ve found an authentication bypass on a system hosting public data. We would certainly emphasize the former in the Executive Summary, but we may not highlight the latter in this section.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L743" data-git-line="743">

      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L744" data-git-line="744">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="744"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 744"

        >744</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Note any trends observed.&lt;ul&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L745" data-git-line="745">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Provide strategic advice.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L746" data-git-line="746">
        <button

          class="git-code-anchor"

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;li&gt;Describe the trends we&amp;#39;ve identified and validate our concerns with summaries of one or two of the more important related findings.&lt;/li&gt;</span>
      </li>

        <button

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L841" data-git-line="841">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="841"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 841"
          title="Copy permalink for line 841"
        >841</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;While this is a technical section, we should not assume the audience is made up of penetration testers. It is better to assume less background knowledge on behalf of the audience and give too much information, rather than the opposite. This section is often presented in tabular form and provides full details of the findings. A finding might cover one vulnerability that has been identified, or may cover multiple vulnerabilities of the same type.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>
    

      <li class="git-code-line" id="L842" data-git-line="842">
        <button
          type="button"
          class="git-code-anchor"
          data-git-line-anchor="842"
          aria-label="Copy permalink for line 842"
          title="Copy permalink for line 842"
        >842</button>
        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;p&gt;It&amp;#39;s important to note that there might be a need for an attack narrative. This narrative describes, in story format, exactly what happened during the test. This is typically done for a simulated threat engagement, but is also useful at times to describe the more complex exploitation steps required for a regular penetration test. If it is necessary, then writing out the attack path step-by-step, with appropriate screenshots, is generally sufficient. An extended narrative could be placed in an Appendix and referenced from the findings table.&lt;/p&gt;</span>
      </li>

        <span class="git-code-text">&lt;td&gt;Malicious JavaScript code can be run to silently carry out malicious activity. A form of this is reflected cross-site scripting (XSS), which occurs when a web application accepts user input with embedded active code and then outputs it into a webpage that is subsequently displayed to a user. This will cause attacker-injected code to be executed on the user&amp;#39;s web browser. XSS attacks can be used to achieve outcomes such as unauthorized access and credential theft, which can in some cases result in reputational and financial damage as a result of bad publicity or fines. As shown in Appendix E.8, the [client] application is vulnerable to an XSS vulnerability because the username value is displayed on the screen login attempt fails. A proof-of-concept using a maliciously crafted username is provided in Appendix E.&lt;/td&gt;</span>
      </li>
